My dear Mr. Secretary
894.74/224
Referring to previous correspondence, and
particularly to my letter of December 22, 1939,
relative to interference to reception of trans-
missions from Station LJY, operating on 17,440
kc, caused by the second harmonic of Station JDK,
operating on 8720 kc, we have been advised by
Press Wireless, Inc., that for the past ten
days this interference has not been present.
Accordingly, it is suggested that no further
representations be made to the Japanese Anthorities
unless the interference recurs.
Your cooperation in eliminating this interference
is greatly appreciated.
Sincerely yours

THE SECRETARY OF STATE.
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's
180a
telegram of September 22, 5 p.m, concerning alleged
Japanese interference with the broadcasts of KGEI,
the General Electric Company's radio station on
Treasure Island in San Francisco Bay, and to my tele-
noon, describing the interference with KGEI experi-
enced in Java. In my telegrams it was stated that
the mechanical interference referred to by the De-
partment was not apparent in Java; that sometimes in-
terference in Morse code, drowning the news broad-
casts, came from stations 7DK and 70A (according to
F/N
the Netherlands Indian Post, Telegraph and Telephone
Service); that in October interference from a con-
tinuous whistle on the exact wave-length of KGEI devel-
oped; and that interference was also being experienced
from a station in Hong Kong.
Since
1

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.96)
- 2 -
Since my last telegram a more serious type of
interference has developed. This comes from some
Japanese broadoasting stations, which, as far as I
jVITJVsjalnax
can understand, are JVI and JVJ, of the Japan Broad-artlyz
2.o
casting Company. At 8.00 p.m., Java time (4.30 a.m.
Pacific Standard Time) these stations broadcast
Japanese news in English on a wave-length which I
would estimate to be around 31.45 or 31.46 meters, or
very close to KGEI's wave-length of 31.48 meters. As
the signals from the Japanese stations are more pow-
erful in Java than those from KGEI, the latter sta-
tion is almost completely drowmed out. The Japanese
stations do not seem to be conneoted with the whistle
referred to in my telegram of October 14, 12 noon, as
the whistle can be turned out of the Japanese signals.
On the other hand, the whistle ceases soon after the
Japanese stations sign off at 10.00 p.m. Java time
(6.30 a.m. Pacific Standard Time). These circum-
stances have led me to believe that it might be pos-
sible that the Japanese have placed the whistle on the
wave-length of KGEI to drown out that station and
broadcast themselves as close to KGEI as possible but
just out of the whistle. In this way they compel lis-
teners in the Far East to abandon attempts to listen
to KGEI and instead to listen to the Japanese version
of the news. But if such is the oase, it cannot be
understood why they sign off at l0.00 p.m. Java time
(6.30 a.m. P.S.T.) and permit people in the Far East
to

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
to listen to the second news broadoast from KGEI.
The second news broadoast from KGEI (6.30 a.m.)
can sometimes be heard fairly distinctly in Java
with a powerful, highly-selective receiving set. For
some weeks past, however, the signals from KGEI seem
to have been weaker than they were before the closing
of the San Francisco Exposition, with the result that
the "side-band interference" from other Far Eastern
stations at times renders KGEI unintelligible, even
after the Japanese stations have signed off. Several
stations located in this part of the world operate on
wave-lengths too close to KGEI to avoid interference.
These include Surabaya (31.41 m.), Bombay (31.41 m.),
Suva (31.46 m.), Calcutta (31.48 m.), Hong Kong (31.49 m.)
and Bangkok (31.55 m.). The latter provides the worst
interference, as it does not stay within its allotted
wave-length but runs several kilocycles over on both
sides.
I have received numerous complaints from residents
of Batavia (principally Americans, British and Dutch), in
regard to the recent difficulty (or the impossibility,
for those with weak receiving sets) of hearing the news
broadoasts from KGEI. News broadcasts from London can
be heard in Java with great distinctness, even with a
amall set, as the beam direoted to Malaya, Australia
and New Zealand passes over Java. The London news,
however, is censored and (perfectly naturally) somewhat
biased. Moreover, it is intended for listeners in the
British possessions and therefore consists in large
part

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
part of so-called "Empire news" and "Home news", which
is of little international interest. German news from
Zessen can also be heard distinotly in Jaga, but as it
consists largely of harangues against British policy and
eulogies of Herr Hitler, few persons take the trouble to
listen to it. Newspaper items distributed through
ANkTA, the local news-agency, consist almost erclusively
of German Transocean (60 per cent) and British Reuter
(40 per cent) despatches. Local residents are therefore
more or less dependent upon KGEI and other American sta-
tions for acourate and impartial news and are greatly
disappointed that the servioe recently is subject to so
much interference. (Strange to say, I have heard more
complaints from Britishers on this score than from
Americans.)
Summarizing the above, it seems that the diffioul-
ties in the reception of KGEI in Java arise from three
causes: (1) that Japanese stations are intentionally or
accidentally interfering; (2) that KGEI signals appear
to have become weaker; and (3) that KGEI is on much too
crowded a wave band to be free from interference in this
part of the world.
I have gone to conaiderable trouble and erpense in
an effort to determine why the reception of KGEI in this
part of the world is as poor as it is. I have rented
powerful Netherlands- and British-made receiving sets
(the only kinds available in the Netherlands Indies),
rigged up various types and sizes of antennae, and
called in the services of erperts. My conclusions are
that

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
that local reception can be improved only if KGEI
either (1) doubles the strength of its signals, or
(2) moves to some less orowded wave-band (the 25-meter
band would be better for this part of the world).
It is realized that KGEI is a non-profit-making sta-
tion; that it is operated solely for the oreation of
international good-will and to afford listeners
abroad with a service which they would not otherwise
receive; and that it cannot be expeoted to expend
large sums of money to improve the service. Therefore
I am simply offering these suggestions in the hope
that something can be done to improve the service of
the station to Malaysia. It should also be remembered
that we are hoping to obtain an open market in the
Netherlands Indies for American radio equipment, both
transmitting and receiving, and that it is not a favor-
able advertisement for such equipment when British and
German programs can be received here much more clearly
than can the most powerfully-sent American program.
Respectfully yours,
Erle R. Dickover,
American Consul General.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
February 20 1940
Receipt is acknowledged of your letter of January 23, 1940, File
894.74/229
No. IN 894.74/reletive to & report received from the American
Consul Generel at Batavia, Java, in regard to interference to the
reception of trensmissions of international broadcast station KGEI,
San Francisco. Your cooperetion in forwarding this informetion is
appreciated.
The Commission is deeply concerned over your feeling thet the
transmissions of Station KGEI do not have priority over tnose of
Station JZI, Japan :nd Station ZBW-3, Hong Kong, because this particu-
lar stetion was placed into operetion later than the Japanese and Hong
Kong stations, even though the United States has a. clear priority on
the notification end first use of the frequency. It has always been
the position of the Commission,  nd, es far :s it is known, the opinion
of other administretions that, other priority determining fectors, such
as the importance of use of the frecuency, the 'use of the frequency in
accordance with international regulations, the technical equipment
installed, the use made of the station, and the power of the station
being equal, the primary factor in determining priority on a frequency
is the notification by the country and its first use by the country.
E
FILED
Due to technical reasons, it is often necessary to change the use of
a frequency from one station to enother in order to use it to the 
greatest efficiency. These changes are periodically necessary for so
ranges of frequencies due to changes in the sunspot cycle. Furtherma,
due to advancement in the art, it is frequently necessary to change the
location of the station to some more suiteble site. At the present
time several of the international broadcast stations are making changes
of this type. These changes will, of course, necessitate a change in
the 3b date shown in the List of Frequencies published by the Bureau of
F/N
the International Telecommunication Union. It would seem inconceivable
that the United States or any other country would lose priority on a
frequency merely because it is used at some different location in order
to obtain the best possible use of the frequency.

=== Page 26 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
In the particular case of 9530 kc, the United States notified
this frequency on March 6, 1930, and it has been in constant use by
the General Electric Company since before l928.
In regard to the weakness of the signal strength of this :tation,
the Commission is making every effort to improve this situation. Rule
4.45 of the Rules and Regulations of the Federal Communications
Commission specifies that after July 1, 1940, no international broad-
cast station will be licensed for operation with a power less then fifty
kilowatts.
It is noted that you have forwerded the informetion conteined in
your letter to the Commission to the Generel Electric Company, and the
Commission will e glad to trrange to discuss this subject vith the
proper officiels of thet Company in an endeavor to arrive at the best
possible solution.
Sincerely yours,
Chairman.

Sir:
With reference to the mbassy's despatch no. 4245
F/A
dated November 20, 1939, concerning interference by
Japanese radio station JZI witi the reception of broad-
casts from Anerican station IGEI, and in confirmetion of
the Embassy's telegram no. 15, January 12, noon, I have
the honor to transmit herewith a translation of a note
from

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
1/
from the Foreign Office, no. 1, American I, dated Jan-
uary 9, 1940, in reply to our representations.
The note includes e report from the appropriate
authorities which states the following in summary:
(1) that station JZI transmitted on the frequency of
9535.425 kilocycles on November 9, 1939, end 9535.390
kilocycles on November 13, which represents a smaller
variation in wave length than that required under inter-
national regulations; (2) that station KGEI, which used
to be W6XBE, did not exist before December 15, 1938 and
that station JZI was registered to a frequency of 9535
kilocycles in September, 1936. Consequently JZI claims
priority and, although no interference by listeners of
station JZI has yet been reported, should there be in-
terference in the future the right is reserved, in ac-
cordance with the provisions of article 35 of the Madrid
Radio Communicetion Treaty and paragraph 2 of article
7 of the general radio communications regulutions supple-
menting that treaty, to re ucst the Americen Government
to take steps to prevent such interference.
Ro ;cetfully yours,
?1
Joseph C. Trew

=== Page 34 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No./ to despatch
No.4/2 dated Jan. /5, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Translation by the American Embassy at Tokyo of a note
verbale in Japansse received from the Foreign Office on
January 10, 1940.
No. 1/ American I.
Ministry of Foreign Affairs,
Tokyo, January 9, 1940.
NOTE VERBALE
The Imperial Ministry of Foreign Affairs presents its
compliments to the American Embassy at Tokyo and has the
honor to acknowledge the receipt of the latter's memoranda
of October 23 and November 15, 1939, concerning elimination
of interference by the Japanese Radio Station JZI, with
reception of radio broadcasts from the American Radio Sta-
tion KGEI. Information contained in the memoranda was
transmitted to the proper governmental authorities with
a request to take appropriate measures after making prompt
investigation. An ad interim reply to this effect, note
No. 162, American I, of December 27,* 1939, has already
been made. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs now has the
honor to forward to the Embassy the following report,
which is the result of the investigations and which has
been received recently from the overnmental authorities
concerned.
Addendum
1. Station JZI transmitted on the frequency of 9535.425
kilocycles on November 9, 1939, and 9535 390 kilocycles on
November 13, with extremely small deviation und a sreat
degree of stability, and furthermore, with the range of
the wave length maintained within a far smaller varietion
than
*Translator's Note: Note No. 162 from the Foreign Office
is dated November 27 and not Deoember 27.

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
than that established by internationel regulations.
2. It is reported that Station KGEI was formerly
WeXBE, and the use of the frequency of 9530 kilocycles of
this station was registered at the Bureau of the Inter-
national Telecommunication Union before Station JZI with
the frequency of 9535 kilocycles, and therefore, it is
asserted, has priority. According to the wave length tables
published by this Bureau, it is clear that Station W6XBE
was first established on December 15, 1938, and did not
exist before that time.
However, 9535 kilocycles of Station JZI was already
registered in the September, 1936, supplement of the
aforementioned tables published by this Bureau and has
been in use since November of that year. Hence, Station
JZI should have priority. Therefors, in this connection,
although reports concerning interference such as given
in the present case from listeners of Station JZI have
not been received, should there be interference in the
future, the right is reserved, in accordance with the
provisions of Article 35 of the Madrid Radio Communication
Treaty and paragraph 2 of Article 7 of the general radio
communication regulations supplementing that treaty, to
request the American Government to take steps to prevent
such interference.

February 7, 1940
Mr. Burke;
This question of Japanese and Hong Kong interferenoe
894.74
with broadoasts from General Eleotrio shortwave station
KGEI, San Francisoo, presents a very difficult and
serious problem. It 1s contended by the Federal Communi-
catione Commission that there is no other suitable fre-
quency to which KGEI may be traneferred.
8
394.74/23
The Commission and the Department differ widely
on the question of priority of registration. To this
Department it appears that the one basis for establishing
priority 1s the date of registration with the Bern Bureau
3
5
and that while the United States has distinot priority
in the original registration of the frequenoy, the
Japanese and Hong Kong stations have a controlling
priority over the use of the frequency by an entirely
new United States station, KGEI. It 1s believed that
some other agencies of this Government agree with that
F/A
restriotion. The Commission argues that other elements
enter into the establiehment of priority such as the
type of equipment installed in the forelgn station,
the importanoe of the frequency for foreign use, the
a ctual use made of the frequenoy by foreign stations,
et celera.

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.95)
-2
et cetera. It would be diffioult, if not utterly impos-
sible, for this Department to inquire into these considera-
tions when interferenoe cases arise.
In view of past discusslons of this matter with the
Commission, I am ignoring the Commission's arguments
in the present instance and am oontenting myeelf with
an expression of interest in the outcome of discussions
between Commission engineers and engineers of the General
Eleotric Company which will possibly result in the use
Towo
of inoreased ruemey by the last-named company. That,
of course, will probably bring a protest from the Japanese.

My dear Mr. Secretary:
Receipt is acknowledged of your letter of February 20, 1940,
894.74/236
File No. IN 894.74/229, relative to interference to the reception
of transmissions of international broadoast station KGEI, San
Francisco, caused by Japanese and Hong Kong broadcasting stations.
As stated in the Commission's letter of January 29, 1940, it is
felt that the United States has a clear priority for operation on
9530 ke. Nevertheless, as it appears that there may be some diffi-
culty in eliminating the interference caused by the Japanese station,
the Commission has been cooper&ting with the General Electric Company
with a view to arriving at a temporary solution to this problem,
pending the elimination of the objectionable interference. It is
felt that with an increase in power of Station KGEI to at least 50
kw as specified by the Rules and Regulations of the Commission for
international broadoast stations after July 1, 1940, some relief will
be afforded. In addition, the Commission is considering the possibilit
of allowing Station KGEI to share time on international broadcast fre-
AR S-1940
FILED
quencies assigned to other licensees. This may be possible without
interfering with the existing service of the other licensees because
of a difference in time.
The Commission appreciates your cooperation in this matter
and trusts that satisfactory service to the Far East by Station KGEI
will soon be made available.

Station JND is reported by the Navy Department at Wash-

frequency 17.665 kilocycles and to be causing severe

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
Erclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 5085 of 0ctober 25, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
The American Embassy to the Japanese Ministry of Foreign
Affairs.
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
URGENT
No. 1658.
The American Embassy presents its compliments to
the Imperial Japanese Ministry of Foreign Affairs and
has the honor to inform it that the Embassy has been
telegraphically apprised thai press wireless station
WJS operating on l5,700 kilocycles is being seriously
interfered with by Japanese station JZ. According to
the Bern Bureau List, frequency 15,700 was notified
on June 28, 1928, by the United States, while Japanese
notification took place only on February 5, 1937.
Moreover, this frequency has been in constant use in
the United States since ay 8, 1931, while no date of
use is shown for this frequency for Japan.
The Embassy would appreciate it if the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs would be good enough to bring this matter
immediately to the attention of the appropriate Japanese
authorities and to request that the necessary measures
be taken to put an end to the interference mentioned.
Tokyo, October 18, 1940.

Enclosure no. 1 to despatch
no. 5134 dated Nov. 13, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Translation by the American Embassy at Tokyo of a note
verbale in Japanese received from the Foreign Office
on November 8, 1940.
MF:C
Ministry of Foreign Affairs,
No.174/American I.
Tokyo.
NOTE VERBALE
The Imperial Ministry of Foreign Affairs presents its
compliments to the American Embassy and has the honor to
acknowledge the receipt of the Embassy's note verbale no. 1658,
October 18, 1940, concerning the elimination of interference
with broadcasts of the American radio station WJS by the
Japanese station JZP. The purport of the above note was
immediately transmitted to the government office concerned.
In this connection, a reply,copy of which is herewith at-
tached, has recently been received from the government of fice
concerned stating that steps have already been taken so that
the cause of interference in question would be completely
eliminated from October 23, 1940.
November 7, 1940.

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Director of American Bureau,
Ministry o1 Foreign Affairs
November 2, 1940.
Director of Electric Affairs Bureau,
Ministry of Communications.
Re Interference with Radio Stations
Acknowledgment is made of your communication of
October 22 concerning the matter mentioned above. An
investigation of the actual circumstances disclosed the
following: The 15,700 kc/s of the Japanese station JZP
has hitherto been the frequency used in communication
with Dairen, and so long as the frequency is used only
for such communication there is no danger that inter-
ference such as that in question will develop. The inter-
ference is believed to have resulted from the recent
temporary use of the said frequency for test communication
with the South Seas Area. In order to eliminate this inter-
ference, and in view of a airect notification received recent-
ly by this office from the New York Wireless Company which
operates station WJS, the use of station JZP for test com-
munication with the South Seas Area was stopped as of
October 23, l940. It is accordingly believed that the
cause of the interference in question has already been
completely eliminated.

ful in removing the disbarment which lay against him.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatch
No. 5211 dated Dec. 13, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Letter from Mr. W. R. Wills to the American Ambassador.
COLUMBIA BROADCASTING SYSTEM, INC.
New York
Japanese Representative:
W. R. Wills,
4, Nichome, Tamuracho,
Shiba-ku, Tokyo.
Hon. Joseph C. Grew,
U. S. Ambassador to Japan,
Tokyo, Japan.
Your Excellency:
As you will undoubtedly recall, I mentioned to you
several weeks ago that Columbia Broadcasting System had
been barred from broadcasting from Japan and that if the
matter were not satisfactorily settled through the regu-
lar channels, I would appreciate your mentioning the sit-
uation to the Minister of Foreign Affairs.
Since nothing appears to have been done by Mr. Ishii
of the Cabinet Information Bureau, to whom I referred the
matter, I am wondering if you would be good enough to
speak to the Minister at your early opportunity.
Roughly the situation is as follows: Columbia requested
me to make a broadcast September 30th, which request I
passed on to Mr. S. Tanomogi, director of the Overseas
Division of the Broadcasting Corporation of Japan. In the
past two years such requests have been granted within 24
hours but in the case under discussion no word was received
for two days. I then called Mr. Tanomogi by telephone but
was

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
was advised that he was not in his office, his assistant
informing me that the request for the broadcast had been
denied by the Cabinet Information Bureau.
I then took the matter up with Mr. Ishii of the Cabinet
Information Bureau and he told me that the request had NOT
been denied by that Bureau but that he would look into the
mattar. Several days later he told me that Mr. Tanomogi
had denied the request on the grounds that Columbia, imme-
diately after my broadcast of August 8th, had made some
remarks disparaging to Japan.
I asked Columbia to cable a transcript of the remarks
immediately following my broadcast to which they replied
at once that Japan had not even been mentioned in the broad-
cast following my own. This I transmitted to Mr. Ishii and
he promised to take the matter up with Mr. Tanomogi. Sev-
eral weeks afterward I again called upon Mr. Ishii and he
told me he had been too busy to see Mr. Tanomogi but that
he would do so the next day. I haven't heard from him since.
By way of explanation I would point out that when
Columbia appointed me as their Far Eastern Representative,
I had some difficulty in inducing Mr. Tanomogi to recognize
me as such. In overcoming his objections I secured the co-
operation of Captain Ogawa (for the past year Naval Attache
to Washington), Captain Mizuno of the Imperial Household
Department and several other influential Japanese friends.
Tanomogi personally called upon me and apologized for hav-
ing refused to deal with me and for the next two years my
relations with him have been confined to making arrangements
for various broadcasts. Not until the most recent matter oc-
curred did I begin to feel that Tanomogi had any resentment
regarding the initial trouble I had with him.
In

=== Page 163 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
In summing up the present situation I feel that Tano-
mogi is using his position to discipline me for forcing
his recognition several years ago. I have never been able
to discover why he holds the views he does as I never knew
the man before I presented my Columbia credentials and my
relations with him since the first trouble was settled,
have apparently been satisfactory.
If the above does not clearly picture the situation, I
shall be glad to answer any questions on any points.
If it would not be too much trouble I would ap preciate
it if you would mention to the Foreign Minister that you
understand Columbia has been barred from broadcasting from
here and that you woulo be interested in knowing the rea-
sons and vhy the matter has not been cleared up.
Assuring you of my appreciation of anything you may do
in this matter, I am
Most respectfully,
(signed)
W. R. WILLS

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3 to despatch
No. 52ll dated Dec. 13, 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Letter from Mr. W. R. Wills to the American Ambassador.
W. R. WILLS
4, Nichome, Tamuracho,
Shiba-ku, Tokyo.
November 20, 1940.
Hon. Joseph C. Grew,
U. S. Ambassador to Japan,
Tokyo, Japan.
Your Excellency:
I am very happy to report that the representations
which you so kindly made on my behalf to the Minister of
894.74
Foreign Affeirs, have begun to bear fruit.
Mr. Junzo Sato of the Foreign Office Information Pureau
266
today informed me that the matter had been taken up with
Mr. Tanomogi of the Overseas Section of the Broadcasting
Corporation of Japan and that hereafter there will be no
trouble in arranging broadcasts.
It seems that Mr. Tanomogi objected to my following the
procedure, which he himself had laid down for me two years
ago, of securing the okay of the War Office, Navy Office
and the Foreign Office on broadcasts, before consulting
with him.
It is needless to say that I am more than willingto
follow his wishes in the matter.
Thank you again for your trouble in the matter. I am
advising Columbia of your most helpful cooperation.
Sincerely,
(signed)
W. R. WILLS

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
Paraphrase of telegram received from Chungking,
dated January 20, 1941.
Secretary of State,
Washington.
Referring to previous communications concerning
reception in the Far East of broadcasts by KGEI.
It has been almost impossible in the recent past to
receive broadcasts from station KGEI. I am informed that
the same is true in other parts of the Far East. The
difficulty seems to be that KGEI uses a frequency in the
31 meter wave band, which band is extensively used by
broadcasts from Rome, London, Moscow, Tokyo, Berlin,
Chungking and Sydney, all of which stations are received
clearly. As a result, KGEI can only be heard in Chungking
late at night.
The stations at Moscow, London and Berlin use other
bands which are less crowded and which come through
effectively esnecially in the 16, 19 and 49 meter bands.
Could KGEI arrange to use other wave lengths during the
earlier part of the evening, since during these times of
crisis its reception is of great use in the Far East as
everybody is most anxious to hear the American point of
view and prefers to receive it from KGEI rather than
through stations at London and Berlin.
JOHNSON.

SHANGHAI RECEPTION OF KGEI
Reception of KGEI, San Francisco, on 9,670 kilocycles
was first noted at Shanghai on July 7, 1940. During
November end December reception was exceptionally good,
even the Saturday football games broadcast on the South
American beam (15,330 kilocycles) were heard.
The
first three weeks in January, 194l reception was
practically nil.
From January 26 to February 1, recep-
tion was fair, but on February 2 was reported as perfect.
Reception then became erratic until by the latter part
of February and first part of llarch it ranged from very
poor to nil, though this may possibly have been due to
sun spots reported as prevailing during this latter
period.
. Reception cleared somewhat about March 4, end
continued to improve until by March 7 and 9 it was very
olear and steady over 9,670 kilocycles from 9:00 o'clock
p.m. (5 a.m. PST) onward.
Reception over 15,330 kilo-
oyoles during this period was heard but faintly.
Frequenoy 9,530 kilocycles broadcast from San
Francisoo between 9:00 p.m. and 11:30 p.m. PST (1:00 p.m.
to 3:30 p.m. Shanghai time) is seldom picked-up at
Shanghai, though in a few instanoes it has been received.
The entire 31 meter band is so crowded that it is
exceptionally difficult to tune out at any time of the
day or evening such stations as Manila, Hong Kong, Japan,
and sometimos Chungking. The 16 and 19 meter bands are
much freer from interference. The 9,670 kilooycle
frequenoy now assigned to KGEI, however, is a distinot
impror

=== Page 189 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
improvement over 9,530 kilocycles from 5 a.m. PST onward.
Shanghai listeners are keenly interested in the
daily news and financial service broadcasts. The American
oommunity eagerly awaits the "Sunday Mail Bag" program,
though the reading of the messages is often too ranid to
permit the full import or text being noted. If the
announcements could be made somewhat slower it would be
appreciated.
Other programs frequently receiving favorable
comments are:
"Information Please"
"Sherlook Holmes"
"One Man's Family"
"Adlrich Family"
"Charlie McCarthy"
"Carnegie Hall Symphony Concerts" such
as given by Toscannini and other
world artists.
1-4/
There is appended hereto reception log notes kindly
furnished by several American radio listeners in Shanghai.
Shanghai Daylight Saving Time comes into force at
midnight, on Saturday, March 15, 1941.
**** *
** * *

Dear Madam:
In persuance to our telephone conversation last
week, I herewith enclose my reception log of KGEI for the
passed seven days. I would like to add at this point that
my receiving location, which is at the Bubbling Well and
Seymour Road district, could not be considered as ideal,
and quite often I receive local interference, whereas at
another location, reception would be clear. Type of
receiver used: G.E. 1940 model, 10 tube.
Wednesday, March 5th.
8:00 p.m. Shanghai time -- Signal can be heard, but
very heavy atmospherics.
9:30 p.m.
Greatly improved, quite clear but with
slight fading and static.
10:30 p.m. - 12:00 p.m. Very good -- at times it was
like a local station.
Thursday, March 6th.
8:00 p.m. Clear signal but not very strong, with
occasional sputtering and fading.
10:00 p.m.
Up till signing off it was very good,
perfect at times.
Friday, March 7th.
8:00 p.m. Signal interfered by local code station.
8:45 p.m. Code stopped -- reception satisfactory.
10:00 p.m. Very good right through.
Saturday. March 8th.
8:00 p.m. Clear with slight background noise.
9:30 p.m. Perfect al1 the way through.
Sunday. March 9th.
Very good reception from start to finish.

FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
May 3, 1941.
Mr. Raymond H. Geist,
Chief, Division of Commercial Affairs,
Department of State,
Washington, D. C.
Subject: Broadcast Reception of Station KGEI in Shanghai.
89
My dear Mr. Geist:
94.74/27
Immodiately upon receipt of radiogram dated March 12, No. 282, we
contacted the General Electric Company and there is quoted below
the contents of reply dated April 22:
"In your letter of April 8 you asked if we had any comments to
pake on the radiogram from Shanghai regarding a KGEI broadcast.
51
"I am quoting below from a letter from Mr. Harris, which is in
answer to this radiogram:
'I am not clear in my own mind as to just what comments
should send Mr. Payne.
 'First of all, we know that we have interference from a
gorman station broadcasting on an assigned frequency of
Department of State
9.675 kc., which is entirely too close to our assigned fre-
COMMERCIAL AFFAIRS
quency of 9, 670 kc. - but this is one of those matters which
will not be straightened out until after the world war is
DIVISION OF
over and there is another international radio convention.
'Some improvement in our service to Asia should be
noticeable when we increase our power from 20 Kw to 50 Kw
about June lst. This increased power combined with highly
efficient permanent antennas, as contrasted with our present
temporary antennas, which are not very efficient because of
0
C
lack of space on Treasure Island, may result in a tery great
improvement, but we will not know this until after we move
/HH
the transmitter to Belmont and increase our power.
'Our mail from Asia, the Antipodes and South Africa
shows this year, the same as last, that in the winter months
we can expect the 31 meter band to lose a great deal of its
efficiency for our broadcasts across the Pacific. We have
gone into this matter thoroughly with the operators of the

=== Page 200 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2,
Mr. Geist,
May 3, 1941.
RCA Communications transmitter at Bolinas, California, where
they have complete records of the efficiency of the various
frequencies for transmitting across the Pacific at various
times of the year, and they strongly recommend that in the
winter months here we should use our 6,190 kc. frequency
for our main Asia broadcasts.
'In his cable, Mr. Lockhart reports that reception of
KGEI on 15,330 kc. was very clear last autumn, but has faded
out almost entirely since that time. This is natural with
the change in the season of the year, but perhaps it should
be pointed out to Mr. Payne that our broadcasts on 15,330 kc.
are directed to Latin America, are not primarily intended for
Asia, and that reception of them in Asia should be considered
more in the line of "freak" reception.
'Mr. Lockhart reports that "KGEI is seldom heard at
Shanghai on the frequency of 9,530 kc. between 9 p.m. and
11:30 p.m. PST. We have had no such broadcasts since last
November. We do broadcast on 9,670 kc. on our Asia beam
from 10 p.m. to 12 midnight PST, and on April 19th will add
another hour to this schedule, broadcasting the Chinese Good
Neighbor Hour from 12 midnight to 1 a.m. PST.
'We were interested to note Mr. Lockhart's comment that
while Shanghai listeners are keenly interested in our Sunday
Mail Bag, the "announcements on this program are too fast to
permit taking note of messages." This is a rather delicate
situation, as we do not at any time wish to have anyone get
the impression that this program is competing with the
commercial cable companies. The messages on this program
are restricted to personal greetings and we have many hundred
reports from China that these messages have been copied dowm
and relayed from one missionary to another. We are receiving
so many messages for this Missionary Mail Bag program that we
are now trimming them down to a maximum of 30 words each, and
even then we have a hard time handl ing them each week. We
have always restricted the rate of talk on our short-wave
broadcasts, believing it better to talk slowly, say less and
be heard, than to talk fast and have an audience miss a great
deal of what we say. However, in view of the fact that this
criticism comes from the State Department, we will immediately
start further slowing up the rate of delivery of the messages
on the Missionary Mail Bag program.'

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 3,
Mr. Geist,
May 3, 1941.
"You may be interested to know that the mail from Asia to KGEI
has shown a big increase in the past few weeks, which would indicate
better reception now than we had in January.
Sincerely yours,
RL Gibson:EP

R.L.G."

are restricted to personal greetings and we have many hundred
reperts from China that these messages have been copied down
and relayed from one missionary to another. We are receiving
so many messages for this Missionary Mail Bag pregram that we
are mow trimming them down to a maximam of 30 words eaeh, and
even then we have a hard time handling them each week. We
have always restricted the rate of talk on our shert-wave
broadeasts, believing it better to talk slowly, say less and
be heard, than to talk fast and have an audience miss a great
deal of what we say. However, in view of the fact that this
criticism comes from the State Department, we will immediately
start further slewing up the rate of telivery of the messages
on the Missionary Mail Bag program.

Spaech: "Actuel Economic Strongth of Japan".
The Tojo Cabinet 1s meeting with great success in making
complete proparations in both the matorial and spiritual spheres.
The country under the leadership of Prime Minister Tojo has be-
come complotely unified and is advencing. I spoke of the two
spheres of the spirituel and material viz. matter ond spirit. It
1s not necessary to state that spirit is the more important. In
the continuation of the "China Incident" for the 3th consocutive
year the downfall of the Chang Kai Shek rogime is graduelly being
brought about. China, England, and America have undor-estinated
the economic strength of Japnn. Actually what is the situntion?
Economic prossure, the freezing of rissets, these have brought
about temporary suffering, but have given us the groatest proof
of tha economic strength of Japen.
The economic strength of the Japeneso poople has been
shown in the "Increusod budget taxation Bill" which has just been
published. While speaking of this increased budget bill, I will
explnin the economic strength of Japan. This bill, which was
published today, is not a bill to oncircle the oxistenco of the
people. It has been formulated after a very thorough study of
"purchase power". It will cause a very high tax in the field
of expensive materials und luxury articles; but for materials
which are necessary for existence the bili is constructed so
as to make them more available for the people, for example: tho
tax on movies has been increased from 2% to 10% but the tax on
necessitius will romcin at 2%. In other words, the bill is to
assist in the carrying out of e livelinood by the populetion.
This bill has been based on a great sympathy for the
people and was published to increase the patriotism of the
populace. e see in the bill evidence of Priener Tojo's Govern-
ment's desire to move forward in full coop:ration with the people.
The recent news has been filled with such things as ∴BCD encircle-
ment and the problems of the Pacific; these things have only
kindled a new spirit in the peopls to fight on villingly and
spiritedly for their Independence. The people today are receiving
orders to report for their conscription, physical examinations,
with an attitude of happiness to be able to fight for their
country.
With the above determination of the people, the policy
of the Government has been firmly established and have recoived
the sanction of the Emperor. Even in the sphere of increased
taxation, there is a unity of Throne, Govornment ond people.
Both directly and indirectly this influence can be seen in the
revamping of taxation system based on assisting the people in
their existence and the new strongth of defense and defense
industries.

LAIRS
2/25/42.
7dew.
The Honorable
894.74/285
The Secretary of State
My dear Mr. Secretary:
894.74
I wish to acknowledge receipt of the Departmentts letter
of February 9, 1942, file IN 894.74/283, and attached copy of
a telegram, dated February 2, 1942, from the American Consulate
General at Sao Paulo requesting this Government's assistance
in jamming broadcasts from Tokyo.
An informal discussion of this matter at the last Defense
Communications Board meeting evolved the opinion that local
Sao Paulo transmitters could perform the most effective
blanketing of Japanese transmissions. Whereas a very low
power transmitter in the immediate area receiving these trans-
missions could effect a satisfactory jamming, it would require
a very high power United States station to create an equiva-
lent interference, with the added disadvantage that if the?
Japanese transmitters changed frequency, it would be some time
before a corresponding change could be made in the United States
transmitters.
It was felt also that in view of the fact that each
country's international broadcast transmissions are extremely
vulnerable, the United States should not embark upon a policy
of jamming such transmissions because of the danger of reprisals.
Sincerely yours,
P
0:
Chairman
B
C
